The electrical outlets, the locks, my tools, even the dust. I keep checking them and fixing them again and again. It takes up my whole day, really. I do it until I'm just completely worn out, but... I still don't feel sure enough to leave it alone. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the actions you're taking to check and fix things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, even when I know for a fact I did a job right, these really ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll get it in my head that a loose wire I missed is going to spark and burn the house down, or... or maybe a nail I left sitting somewhere is going to hurt my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they always come right back, and they usually feel even stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying a minute ago, that's the whole reason I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools... even the dust. I feel like I have to keep checking and fixing them over and over to stop those bad things from happening. I just keep at it until I'm completely worn out, but... I still never really feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of bad things happening would actually come true if you didn't check and fix things?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I'm not sure if they actually would come true or not. It's... it's hard to say, because I never really let it get that far. I just know the feeling in my head is so strong that I can't take the chance. So whether a fire would actually start or someone would actually get hurt... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just know I have to stop it just in case.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's all about making sure my wife doesn't get hurt or that a fire doesn't start. Like I was saying, that's the reason I end up spending most of my day just going back over those same outlets, the locks, the tools... even the dust. I just check and fix them again and again until I'm totally worn out. And the worst part is, even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I don't really care if things are lined up or even. It's not about putting things in a specific order for me or making them look a certain way. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. So, no, I don't have that feeling about symmetry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking and fixing actions a specific number of times to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about counting or a specific number. I don't, like, tell myself I have to check the lock three times or anything like that. It's more about the feeling. I just keep checking and fixing until it feels right... or until I'm just too exhausted to keep going. But there's no set number I'm trying to reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing things helps reduce your anxiety about potential harm?\n\nPatient: I guess that's what I'm trying to do, yeah. But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I spend most of my day just going back over the same things in my house. I still don't feel sure enough to leave it alone. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the actions you're taking to check and fix things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, even when I know for a fact I did a job right, these really ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll get it in my head that a loose wire I missed is going to spark and burn the house down, or... or maybe a nail I left sitting somewhere is going to hurt my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they always come right back, and they usually feel even stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying a minute ago, that's the whole reason I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools... even the dust. I feel like I have to keep checking and fixing them over and over to stop those bad things from happening. I just keep at it until I'm completely worn out, but... I still never really feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of bad things happening would actually come true if you didn't check and fix things?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I'm not sure if they actually would come true or not. It's... it's hard to say, because I never really let it get that far. I just know the feeling in my head is so strong that I can't take the chance. So whether a fire would actually start or someone would actually get hurt... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just know I have to stop it just in case.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's all about making sure my wife doesn't get hurt or that a fire doesn't start. I just check and fix them again and again until I'm totally worn out. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Um... I don't really care if things are lined up or even. It's not about putting things in a specific order for me or making them look a certain way. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. So, no, I don't have that feeling about symmetry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking and fixing actions a specific number of times to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about counting or a specific number. I don't, like, tell myself I have to check the lock three times or anything like that. It's more about the feeling. I just keep checking and fixing until it feels right... or until I'm just too exhausted to keep going. But there's no set number I'm trying to reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing things helps reduce your anxiety about potential harm?\n\nPatient: I guess that's what I'm trying to do, yeah. But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your main concern is making a mistake that would lead to harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll start thinking that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down, or that a nail I left somewhere is going to end up hurting my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I spend most of my day just going back over the same things in my house. The electrical outlets, the locks, my tools, even the dust. I keep checking them and fixing them again and again. It takes up my whole day, really. I do it until I'm just completely worn out, but... I still don't feel sure enough to leave it alone. I just can't stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the actions you're taking to check and fix things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's... well, even when I know for a fact I did a job right, these really ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll get it in my head that a loose wire I missed is going to spark and burn the house down, or... or maybe a nail I left sitting somewhere is going to hurt my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they always come right back, and they usually feel even stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying a minute ago, that's the whole reason I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools... even the dust. I feel like I have to keep checking and fixing them over and over to stop those bad things from happening. I just keep at it until I'm completely worn out, but... I still never really feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of bad things happening would actually come true if you didn't check and fix things?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I'm not sure if they actually would come true or not. It's... it's hard to say, because I never really let it get that far. I just know the feeling in my head is so strong that I can't take the chance. So whether a fire would actually start or someone would actually get hurt... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just know I have to stop it just in case.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's all about making sure my wife doesn't get hurt or that a fire doesn't start. Like I was saying, that's the reason I end up spending most of my day just going back over those same outlets, the locks, the tools... even the dust. I just check and fix them again and again until I'm totally worn out. And the worst part is, even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I don't really care if things are lined up or even. It's not about putting things in a specific order for me or making them look a certain way. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. So, no, I don't have that feeling about symmetry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking and fixing actions a specific number of times to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about counting or a specific number. I don't, like, tell myself I have to check the lock three times or anything like that. It's more about the feeling. I just keep checking and fixing until it feels right... or until I'm just too exhausted to keep going. But there's no set number I'm trying to reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing things helps reduce your anxiety about potential harm?\n\nPatient: I guess that's what I'm trying to do, yeah. But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your main concern is making a mistake that would lead to harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll start thinking that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down, or that a nail I left somewhere is going to end up hurting my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up.

But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your main concern is making a mistake that would lead to harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll start thinking that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down, or that a nail I left somewhere is going to end up hurting my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are a part of you or something that's being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean... they're happening in my own head, so I guess they're my thoughts. But I definitely don't want them there. \n\nIt's not like I feel like someone else is putting them in my brain, if that's what you mean. It's more just... my own mind getting stuck on things. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it. They just show up whether I want them to or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because I spend most of my day just going back over the same things in my house. The electrical outlets, the locks, my tools, even the dust. I keep checking them and fixing them again and again. It takes up my whole day, really. I do it until I'm just completely worn out, but... It's... well, even when I know for a fact I did a job right, these really ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll get it in my head that a loose wire I missed is going to spark and burn the house down, or... or maybe a nail I left sitting somewhere is going to hurt my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they always come right back, and they usually feel even stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing these things is necessary to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. Like I was saying a minute ago, that's the whole reason I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools... even the dust. I feel like I have to keep checking and fixing them over and over to stop those bad things from happening. I just keep at it until I'm completely worn out, but... I still never really feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts of bad things happening would actually come true if you didn't check and fix things?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I mean... I'm not sure if they actually would come true or not. It's... it's hard to say, because I never really let it get that far. I just know the feeling in my head is so strong that I can't take the chance. So whether a fire would actually start or someone would actually get hurt... I really couldn't tell you for sure. I just know I have to stop it just in case.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's all about making sure my wife doesn't get hurt or that a fire doesn't start. Like I was saying, that's the reason I end up spending most of my day just going back over those same outlets, the locks, the tools... even the dust. I just check and fix them again and again until I'm totally worn out. And the worst part is, even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I don't really care if things are lined up or even. It's not about putting things in a specific order for me or making them look a certain way. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. So, no, I don't have that feeling about symmetry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking and fixing actions a specific number of times to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about counting or a specific number. I don't, like, tell myself I have to check the lock three times or anything like that. It's more about the feeling. I just keep checking and fixing until it feels right... or until I'm just too exhausted to keep going. But there's no set number I'm trying to reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing things helps reduce your anxiety about potential harm?\n\nPatient: I guess that's what I'm trying to do, yeah. But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are a part of you or something that's being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean... they're happening in my own head, so I guess they're my thoughts. But I definitely don't want them there. \n\nIt's not like I feel like someone else is putting them in my brain, if that's what you mean. It's more just... my own mind getting stuck on things. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it. They just show up whether I want them to or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something you want to have, or are they unwelcome and something you wish you could get rid of?\n\nPatient: I definitely don't want them. I'd give anything to get rid of them. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned earlier... I'll know for a fact I did a job right, but those ugly thoughts just keep jumping in anyway. The ones about a loose wire I might have missed burning the house down, or my wife stepping on a nail I supposedly left out. I hate thinking about that stuff. I try my absolute best to just push them out of my head, but... it's like the more I try to block them out, they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, they are completely unwelcome.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I was saying a minute ago, that's the whole reason I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools... even the dust. I mean... I'm not sure if they actually would come true or not. It's... it's hard to say, because I never really let it get that far. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your main concern is making a mistake that would lead to harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll start thinking that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down, or that a nail I left somewhere is going to end up hurting my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are a part of you or something that's being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean... they're happening in my own head, so I guess they're my thoughts. But I definitely don't want them there. \n\nIt's not like I feel like someone else is putting them in my brain, if that's what you mean. It's more just... my own mind getting stuck on things. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it. They just show up whether I want them to or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something you want to have, or are they unwelcome and something you wish you could get rid of?\n\nPatient: I definitely don't want them. I'd give anything to get rid of them. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned earlier... I'll know for a fact I did a job right, but those ugly thoughts just keep jumping in anyway. The ones about a loose wire I might have missed burning the house down, or my wife stepping on a nail I supposedly left out. I hate thinking about that stuff. I try my absolute best to just push them out of my head, but... it's like the more I try to block them out, they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, they are completely unwelcome.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they're definitely not. I mean, I love my wife. I don't want anything bad to happen to her, and I certainly don't want to be the cause of it. \n\nI take pride in my work, you know? I want to be safe and responsible. So having these thoughts that I'm being careless or that I'm going to cause a fire or hurt someone... it goes against everything I believe in. That's why it bothers me so much. It's like my brain is trying to convince me I'm this reckless person, and I know I'm not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just know I have to stop it just in case.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It's all about making sure my wife doesn't get hurt or that a fire doesn't start. Like I was saying, that's the reason I end up spending most of my day just going back over those same outlets, the locks, the tools... even the dust. I just check and fix them again and again until I'm totally worn out. And the worst part is, even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um... I don't really care if things are lined up or even. It's not about putting things in a specific order for me or making them look a certain way. It's really just... just what I was saying before, about making sure things are safe. So, no, I don't have that feeling about symmetry or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checking and fixing actions a specific number of times to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about counting or a specific number. I don't, like, tell myself I have to check the lock three times or anything like that. It's more about the feeling. I just keep checking and fixing until it feels right... or until I'm just too exhausted to keep going. But there's no set number I'm trying to reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking and fixing things helps reduce your anxiety about potential harm?\n\nPatient: I guess that's what I'm trying to do, yeah. But honestly... it doesn't really work that well. Like I was saying earlier, I end up spending most of my day just going back over the exact same things—the outlets, the locks, the tools, the dust. \n\nI just keep checking and fixing them again and again. It might make me feel better for a split second, but then the worry comes right back. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it doesn't really get rid of the anxiety, it just takes over my whole day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors are aimed at making things perfect or 'just right'?\n\nPatient: No, it's not really about perfection. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's not about making it perfect, it's just trying to make it safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your main concern is making a mistake that would lead to harm?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly it. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I'll start thinking that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down, or that a nail I left somewhere is going to end up hurting my wife. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. So, yeah, I'm just terrified I missed something that's going to cause real harm.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would be responsible if something bad happened as a result of not checking or fixing these things?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, absolutely. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. So it really just comes back to being afraid of what will happen if I don't catch it.\n\nDoctor: Are your recurring thoughts more about causing harm to others or yourself, or are they about something being contaminated or unclean?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about harm. I don't really worry about germs or things being unclean. Like I was saying a minute ago, it's those ugly thoughts jumping in about... well, a loose wire burning the house down or my wife getting hurt by a nail I left out. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are a part of you or something that's being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean... they're happening in my own head, so I guess they're my thoughts. But I definitely don't want them there. \n\nIt's not like I feel like someone else is putting them in my brain, if that's what you mean. It's more just... my own mind getting stuck on things. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it. They just show up whether I want them to or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something you want to have, or are they unwelcome and something you wish you could get rid of?\n\nPatient: I definitely don't want them. I'd give anything to get rid of them. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned earlier... I'll know for a fact I did a job right, but those ugly thoughts just keep jumping in anyway. The ones about a loose wire I might have missed burning the house down, or my wife stepping on a nail I supposedly left out. I hate thinking about that stuff. I try my absolute best to just push them out of my head, but... it's like the more I try to block them out, they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, they are completely unwelcome.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they're definitely not. I mean, I love my wife. I don't want anything bad to happen to her, and I certainly don't want to be the cause of it. \n\nI take pride in my work, you know? I want to be safe and responsible. So having these thoughts that I'm being careless or that I'm going to cause a fire or hurt someone... it goes against everything I believe in. That's why it bothers me so much. It's like my brain is trying to convince me I'm this reckless person, and I know I'm not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something that you would normally think, or are they completely out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They're completely out of character. Like I said, I'm a careful guy. I care about doing things right and keeping my family safe. So no, I wouldn't normally think about leaving a wire loose or a nail on the floor. That's just... it's not who I am or how I operate. Before all this started getting so bad, I never worried about this kind of stuff. I'd just finish a job and go home. Now it's like I don't even recognize my own thought process sometimes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I do it until I'm completely worn out, and even after all that, I still don't feel sure enough to just leave it alone. I don't care if things look perfect or... or even if they're exactly right in that way. It's really just about the danger. I'm just trying to make sure nothing catches on fire or... or that someone doesn't break in. It's all about making a mistake. Even when I know I did a job right, these... these ugly thoughts just keep jumping in. I try to just push them out of my head, but they come right back stronger. If something bad happened because I didn't check it, it would completely be my fault. I mean, I'm the one who knows it might be dangerous. If I just walked away and my wife got hurt, or the house burned down... that's on me. I couldn't live with that. So, yeah, I definitely feel like I'd be the one responsible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're driven to check and fix things because you're afraid of what might happen if you don't, or because you're afraid of making a mistake itself?\n\nPatient: I don't really know how to separate those two things. To me, making a mistake means something bad is going to happen. So... I guess I'm afraid of both? \n\nLike, I'm terrified of messing up, but only because messing up means a fire starts or my wife gets hurt. If making a mistake didn't cause any harm, I don't think I'd care about it at all. Even when I know I did the job perfectly fine, they just keep coming. I try to push them out of my head, but they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, it's always about the fear of someone getting hurt, not contamination.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about harm and danger are nonsense, or do they seem reasonable to you?\n\nPatient: Well, that's the frustrating part. Deep down, I know they're nonsense. I mean, I know I put the tools away. I know I unplugged the saw. It's completely unreasonable to think that somehow a nail just magically appeared on the floor where my wife could step on it when I know I swept up. \n\nBut... in the moment, when the thought hits me, it feels so real. It feels like a genuine warning. So my logical brain knows it's ridiculous, but the feeling of danger is so strong that I just can't ignore it. I have to go check, even though I know it doesn't make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are a part of you or something that's being imposed on you?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean... they're happening in my own head, so I guess they're my thoughts. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it. They just show up whether I want them to or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something you want to have, or are they unwelcome and something you wish you could get rid of?\n\nPatient: I definitely don't want them. I'd give anything to get rid of them. \n\nI mean, like I mentioned earlier... I'll know for a fact I did a job right, but those ugly thoughts just keep jumping in anyway. The ones about a loose wire I might have missed burning the house down, or my wife stepping on a nail I supposedly left out. I hate thinking about that stuff. I try my absolute best to just push them out of my head, but... it's like the more I try to block them out, they just come right back stronger. So, yeah, they are completely unwelcome.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are in line with your own values and beliefs?\n\nPatient: No, they're definitely not. I mean, I love my wife. I don't want anything bad to happen to her, and I certainly don't want to be the cause of it. \n\nI take pride in my work, you know? I want to be safe and responsible. So having these thoughts that I'm being careless or that I'm going to cause a fire or hurt someone... it goes against everything I believe in. That's why it bothers me so much. It's like my brain is trying to convince me I'm this reckless person, and I know I'm not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about harm and danger are something that you would normally think, or are they completely out of character for you?\n\nPatient: They're completely out of character. Like I said, I'm a careful guy. I care about doing things right and keeping my family safe. So no, I wouldn't normally think about leaving a wire loose or a nail on the floor. I wouldn't say I'm choosing to think them, but I know it's my own brain doing it. So I don't really know how to classify it.
